(2) Difference hypotheses -sensitivity: Learners in the low-generative worked example condition will show more elaborated deep-feature knowledge in the think-aloud measure (2a), the recognition test (2b), and the stimulated self-report (2c) than learners in the high-generative invention condition.

(3) Regression hypotheses -predictive validity: Learners' intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure (3a), the recognition test (3b), and stimulated self-report (3c) predicts learning outcomes (near and far transfer).foot_0

Method

Participants and Design

The study followed a one-factorial design, with the generativity of the preparation activity as the between-subjects factor. Participants were randomly assigned to one of the two experimental conditions. They either invented a solution (high-generative, n = 35) or selfexplained a worked-out solution (low-generative, n = 34). All participants were psychology students at a German university in their first semester of a Bachelor's degree program. They received course credit for their participation and gave informed consent before taking part. We measured intermediate deep-feature knowledge in three ways (think-aloud, recognition test, and stimulated self-report). The experiment was approved by the university's ethics board and preregistered at OSF (https://osf.io/2j6t8/overview?view_only=81b5529f0e074d2cb437a8c354f2ecba).

We expected a large effect size for the correlational hypotheses (r = .50), a medium effect size for the regression hypotheses (R² = .12), and d = 0.60 (based on Glogger-Frey et al., 2022) for the difference hypotheses. Based on these a priori power considerations, the target sample size was N = 100 in order to achieve at least .90 power. Initially, 77 first-year students participated in the experiment. We excluded eight participants due to technical issues or problems with the think-aloud procedure or preregistered criteria, namely, a reported lack of commitment, resulting in 69 valid cases (83% female; 16% male, 1% nonbinary; age: M = 20.2, SD = 2.4). With N = 69, we could still achieve an acceptable power of ≥ .80 for all analyses.

Materials and Measurement

Preparation Task. The learning material for the preparation phase was experimentally varied. In both experimental groups, the material consisted of three contrasting cases in the expect similar result patterns for each deep-feature measure and will interpret this similarity as an indicator of convergent validity. However, we failed to preregister separate hypotheses for the stimulated self-report and the recognition test and originally preregistered only the following regression hypotheses: We expect that learners' deep-feature recognition (measured with the think-aloud procedure) will predict the learning outcomes (near transfer, H3a; far transfer, H3b).

form of diagrams (based on Wiedmann et al., 2012). In each diagram, the antioxidant measurements of a fictional tea-grower's tea were depicted as data points, which differed systematically in variability between the diagrams (see Figure 1). Participants in the highgenerative group were asked to intuitively rate the variability of the presented data first, before inventing a mathematical method to describe and calculate the variabilities. The low-generative worked-example version was taken from Ockl and Glogger-Frey (2026), following the principles of example-based learning (Renkl, 2014). It demonstrated the invention process of a fictional classmate, including typical erroneous solutions, the canonical solution, and self-explanation prompts. Participants were instructed to self-explain the presented solution and give the thoughts of a fictional classmate instead of producing a solution on their own (cf. Glogger-Frey et al., 2015; Glogger-Frey et al., 2017; Schalk et al., 2018). Online Resource 1 presents the entire list of preparation tasks. Note. Contrasting cases differing in aspects connected to the deep features: number of data The think-aloud measure was coded according to the procedure recommended by Chi (1997). Before coding, all expressed thoughts were recorded and transcribed using the AI-based transcription software noScribe (Dröge, 2023). Student assistants carefully checked and corrected the raw transcripts generated by the software. They then coded the following deep features: (a) inclusion of all data points, (b) deviations from the mean (fixed reference point), (c) squared or absolute deviations, and (d) dividing by the number of data points. The deep features were derived from the four functional components of the standard deviation formula described by Loibl et al. (2020). The level of processing of each deep feature was coded on a scale ranging from 0 (not mentioned) to 4 (elaboration + correct mathematical solution) within the protocols as specified by the rating scheme described in Table 1. The final think-aloud score for intermediate deep-feature knowledge was the number of deep features that were processed at a level of elaboration (Level 3) or higher. About 25% of the think-aloud protocol were doublecoded by three trained student assistants (ICC = .72).

The recognition test presented defamiliarised variants of the original diagrams from the preparation activity. Each recognition test item targeted one deep feature derived from the functional components of the standard deviation formula described in Loibl et al. (2020)foot_1 and was based on the same principle: Each item consisted of three defamiliarised sets of diagrams and only one of them correctly represented the targeted deep feature (e.g., different number of data points as an approximation of the deep feature 'divide by N'), besides the other deep features. The remaining two sets of diagrams depicted only the other deep features correctly but not the targeted deep features. Figure 2 presents an example of a recognition test item.

Participants were asked to decide which set of diagrams most closely resembled the original diagrams from the preparation task and to briefly provide their reasoning for their decision. Each deep feature was tested by one item, and participants could receive up to 2 points per deep feature (1 for a correct decision and 1 for the correct reasoning). The coding of the reasoning followed a rating scheme (see Table 2). Two independent raters double-coded 25% (ICC = .92).

Altogether, the test consisted of three items, and participants could receive up to 6 points.

Online Resource 2 presents the full recognition test. Note. Item from the recognition test showing three defamiliarised diagram sets. Whereas all diagram sets correctly depict two deep features by showing different numbers of data points and the same range of the distributions on the left and the right, only the first diagram set also correctly depicts the targeted deep feature 'deviation from the mean' by showing different distributions around the centre, as in the original. Translated by the first author. The stimulated self-report asked participants to rate their processing of deep features in a self-report questionnaire at the end of the experiment (after the posttest). In the questionnaire, each of the four deep features and its relevance were described in two or three sentences, and participants were asked if and when they had thought about this aspect for the first time, for example: 'When estimating the variance of a distribution, it is not sufficient to consider only the distribution's extreme points, as this will not produce accurate results. Therefore, it is important to consider all data points to get an accurate estimate. Please think back to the learning sequence. At what point did you first consider the aspect that all data points must be considered?' To stimulate the recall, each study phase was described in the answer options: 'during the task with the tea growers and the think-aloud' -'during the rating of the material and task with the tea growers' -'during the recognition task (where you had to recognise the diagrams)' -'during the exploratory videos' -'while working through the exercises' -'I did not think about this aspect'.

Participants gave their answer by choosing one of these options.

Because only the deep features considered before the subsequent learning phase were of interest, the final score was calculated by adding up the number of deep features reported as being considered during the preparation activity (i.e., the first answer option). Altogether, the self-report consisted of four items, and participants could receive up to 4 points. Online

Resource 3 presents the entire stimulated self-report.

Instructional Video.

A brief instructional video (~ 8 minutes, presentation slides with voiceover) was presented in the instructional phase after the preparation phase. The video covered different measures of dispersion -namely, range, mean absolute deviation (MAD), variance, and standard deviation, with a focus on fostering a conceptual understanding of the variance and standard deviation. The video used the example from the preparation task, showed how to calculate the different measures, and explained their advantages and disadvantages.

Posttest. Learning outcomes were measured with a posttest, including five items targeting near transfer and two items targeting the far transfer of conceptual knowledge used in Ockl & Glogger-Frey (2026; based on Kapur, 2012; Newman & DeCaro, 2019; Trninic et al., 2022).

The five near-transfer items (four single-choice and one open-ended) required, inter alia, the interpretation of variance values, for example, 'Two cinema owners argue about whose cinema has more consistent attendance. They record the daily attendance for a month -the results are shown below. Which cinema has the more consistent attendance?' The two far-transfer items were open-ended and required the use of conceptual knowledge about measures of variance for argumentation, for example, 'The table shows the points scored by members of two training groups. The two highest-scoring members achieved the same score. Which group has the most outstanding athlete?' The open items were scored by two independent raters following a rating scheme (all ICCs > .67). All discrepancies were discussed by the raters, and a joint decision was made. Online Resource 4 presents the posttest and the rating scheme.

Prior Knowledge and Commitment. Participants' self-reported commitment while working on the tasks was assessed as a form of manipulation check. We asked once after the preparation and once after the posttest how committed they felt while working on the tasks:

Procedure

The study was conducted in soundproof cabins in the laboratory. At the beginning, a trained student assistant introduced participants to the think-aloud procedure, and they were asked to think aloud following the procedure suggested by Ericsson and Simon (1980). The preparation tasks were presented after a brief practice phase to familiarise participants with the think-aloud procedure. Participants had 25 minutes to work on the tasks while speaking aloud all their thoughts. The utterances were recorded, and a student assistant monitored the thinkaloud process and reminded participants to keep talking if necessary ('please keep talking'; no explanation prompted etc., see Fox et al., 2011). Then, participants self-assessed their commitment while working on the tasks and completed the recognition test, which was presented on the computer. After a short break, the instructional video was presented on the computer screen, followed by the self-assessment of prior knowledge and the demographic questionnaire. Then participants had 20 minutes to complete the posttest with single-choice items presented on the computer screen and open items presented in a paper-pencil format.

Finally, participants completed the stimulated self-report.

Results

The datasets analysed during the studies are available in the Zenodo repository (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22689891). A significance level of .05 was used with all analyses. Effect sizes were expressed by d, r, or R² and classified according to Cohen (1988) as small (d = .20; r = .10; R² = .02), medium (d = .50; r = .30; R² = .13), or large (d = .80; r = .50; R² = .26).

Preliminary Analyses

We tested for systematic differences between conditions regarding prior knowledge and self-reported commitment. We found no significant differences in learners' self-rated prior knowledge regarding the variance formula or measures of dispersion, both Us < 595.00, both Zs < -0.24, both ps > .809 (two-tailed). Learners' self-reported commitment during preparation and while working on the posttest was high (see Table 3) and did not differ between conditions, both ts (67) < -1.21, both ps > .234 (two-tailed). Table 3 presents the means and standard deviations of the intermediate deep-feature knowledge measures, and posttest per condition. Table 4 presents the intercorrelations of these variables. Table 5 shows the frequency statistics for the stimulated self-report. Note. Values represent frequencies in percent.

Convergent Validity (Hypotheses 1a and 1b)

We expected intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure to be strongly positively correlated with the recognition test score (H1a) and the stimulated self-report score (H1b). Deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure was significantly correlated with the stimulated self-report, r = .27, p = .014 (onetailed), but not with recognition test, r = .19, p = .060 (one-tailed). Further analyses within conditions revealed no difference in the result patterns for the recognition test (low-generative: r = .23, p = .098; high-generative: r = .26, p = .065). But we found differences in the result pattern for the (overall significantly related) stimulated self-report score with a significant correlation only appearing in the high-generative condition, r = .48, p = .002, but not in the low-generative condition, r = .13, p = .237.

As the correlation patterns between the self-report and the think-aloud measure differed between conditions, we conducted further exploratory analyses. Table 5 shows the frequency statistics for the stimulated self-report and reveals that, unlike the high-generative group, 23-35% of participants in the low-generative condition reported elaborating on a deep feature during the questionnaire after preparation. This result is unconvincing given that the questionnaire only assessed (unspecific) knowledge-gap awareness and commitment during preparation. It seems more sensible that learners in the low-generative condition struggled to differentiate between the two phases. Consequently, we calculated an alternative self-report score that counted both options as elaboration during preparation. The correlation between the think-aloud score and the new self-report score was significant, r = .52, p < .001 (one-tailed), and we found similar result patterns for both the high-generative, r = .43, p = .045, and the lowgenerative conditions, r = .37, p = .017.

Differences Between Preparation Activities -Sensitivity (Hypotheses 2a, 2b, and 2c)

We expected low-generative preparation to result in greater deep-feature knowledge than high-generative preparation and for this difference to be detected in the think-aloud measure (H2a), recognition test (H2b), and stimulated self-report (H2c). The low-generative condition elaborated significantly more deep features than the high-generative condition in the think-aloud measure, t(67) = -4.08, p < .001 (one-tailed), d = -0.98, but this difference was not detected with the recognition test, t(67) = 0.42, p = .338 (one-tailed), d = 0.04, or the (original) stimulated self-report, t(67) = -0.87, p = .194 (one-tailed), d = -0.21 (see Table 3 for descriptives).

However, the exploratory stimulated self-report score showed the same result pattern as the think-aloud measure with more deep features reported after a low-generative, M = 2.82, SD = 1.17, compared with a high-generative preparation activity, M = 1.60, SD = 1.07, t(67) = -4.56, p < .001 (one-tailed), d = -1.10.

Regression -Predictive Validity (Hypotheses 3a and 3b)

We expected that learners' intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure (3a), the recognition test (3b), and the stimulated self-report (3c) would predict learning outcomes (near and far transfer). The think-aloud measure did not significantly predict near transfer, F(1, 67) = 3.25, β = .22, p = .076, adjusted R² = .03, or far transfer, F(1, 67) = 2.99, β = .21, p = .089, adjusted R² = .03. However, separate analyses for the subgroups revealed that the think-aloud measurement significantly predicted near transfer within the low-generative condition, F(1, 32) = 6.42, β = .41, p = .016, adjusted R² = .14, but not within the high-generative condition, F(1, 33) = 1.22, β = .19, p = .277, adjusted R² = .01. The recognition test could not significantly predict near transfer F(1, 67) = 0.54, β = .09, p = .467, adjusted R² < .01, or far transfer, F(1, 67) = 0.04, β = .03, p = .839, adjusted R² < .01. Separate analyses for the subgroups revealed no difference in the result patterns. The stimulated self-report did not significantly predict near transfer, F(1, 67) = 1.36, β = .14, p = .248, adjusted R² = .01, or far transfer, F(1, 67) = 0.14, β = -.05, p = .710, adjusted R² < .01. Separate analyses for the subgroups revealed no difference in the result patterns.

The exploratory stimulated self-report scores did not significantly predict near transfer F(1, 67) = 1.20, β = .13, p = .278, adjusted R² = .03, or far transfer, F(1, 67) = 0.51, β = .09, p = .480, adjusted R² < .01. However, separate analyses for the subgroups revealed that the new scores predicted near transfer more strongly within the low-generative condition, F(1, 32) = 3.61, β = .32, p = .066, adjusted R² = .07, than within the high-generative condition, F(1, 33) = 0.78, β = .15, p = .383, adjusted R² = < .01, results that are similar to the results for the think-aloud measure.

Further Exploratory Analyses

In further exploratory analyses, we were interested in the correlations between the recognition test subscores (decision subscore, reasoning subscore) and the think-aloud measure. Ideally, the test would not prompt learners to explain because such prompted reasoning processes potentially deepen emerging knowledge. The correct-decision subscore was significantly correlated with the think-aloud score, but the reasoning subscore was not (see Table 6).

Discussion Study 1

In line with previous studies (Glogger-Frey et al., 2022; Ockl & Glogger-Frey, 2026; Roelle & Berthold, 2015), Study 1 replicated the finding that low-generative preparation with a worked example results in better intermediate deep-feature knowledge than high-generative preparation with an invention task, as measured by the think-aloud procedure. However, this difference was not observed in either the recognition test or the stimulated self-report, indicating poor sensitivity for these instruments. Looking more closely at the recognition test, we found only a small, nonsignificant correlation with the think-aloud measure, which also suggests poor convergent validity. However, exploratory analyses revealed significant, medium correlations between the decision subscore and the think-aloud measure but not between the reasoning subscore and the think-aloud measure. This discrepancy between the subscores might be due to learners' lack of appropriate (statistical) vocabulary to describe the reasoning process.

Participants may therefore have struggled to explain their decision even if their reasoning was correct. As the reasoning part did not add anything to the recognition test and is more prone to inducing reactivity (deepening emerging knowledge), we removed it from a subsequent study (Study 2).

Looking more closely at the stimulated self-report, we found a medium, significant correlation with the think-aloud measure. Although this finding indicates better convergent validity than the recognition test, the correlation was still not strong enough (r < .50) to serve as a proper alternative to the think-aloud procedure because lower convergent validity is associated with an increase in divergent findings between measurements (Carlson & Herdman, 2012).

Exploratory analyses suggested that the formulation of the self-report answer options may be misleading. Participants in the low-generative condition in particular seemed to struggle to differentiate between the phases described in the first two answer options. This finding also suggests that the text-based stimulation was insufficient for stimulating participants' recall and, consequently, their self-reports. Therefore, we modified and adapted the stimulated self-report for Study 2.

Study 2

The aim of Study 2 was to test adapted versions of the instruments used in Study 1 to see if the adjustments had been effective. We tested essentially the same hypotheses as in Study 1, (preregistered under https://osf.io/wajne/overview?view_only=9f4ae2374f204f69bb861d041db8a398): (1) Correlational hypotheses -convergent validity: Intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure will be strongly positively correlated with the recognition test score (1a) and the stimulated self-report score(1b).

(2) Difference hypotheses -sensitivity: Learners in the low-generative worked example condition will show more elaborated deep-feature knowledge in the think-aloud measure (2a), recognition test (2b), and stimulated self-report (2c) than learners in the high-generative invention condition.

(3) Regression hypotheses -predictive validity: Learners' intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure (3a), the recognition test (3b), and the stimulated self-report (3c) predicts learning outcomes.

Method

Participants and Design

As in Study 1, the study followed a one-factorial design with preparation activity as the between-subjects factor. Participants were randomly assigned to one of the two experimental conditions and either invented a solution (high-generative, n = 19) or self-explained a worked-out solution (low-generative, n = 20). As in Study 1, all participants were psychology students at a German university in the first semester of a Bachelor's degree program and received course credit for their participation. All participants gave informed consent before taking part. The experiment was approved by the university's ethics board and preregistered at OSF (https://osf.io/wajne/overview?view_only=9f4ae2374f204f69bb861d041db8a398). We expected large effects for the correlational hypotheses (r = .50), R² = .09 (based on Glogger-Frey et al., 2015) and the regression hypotheses and d = 0.60 (based on Glogger-Frey et al., 2022) for the difference hypotheses. On the basis of these a priori power considerations, the targeted sample size was N = 90 in order to achieve at least .83 power in all analyses. Initially, 44 first-year students participated in the experiment, but we had to exclude 5 participants due to technical issues, problems with the think-aloud procedure, or preregistered criteria, namely, a reported lack of commitment, resulting in only 39 valid cases (82% female; 10% male, 8% nonbinary; age: M = 20.9, SD = 3.2). Therefore, our sample size was much smaller than planned, but it did not affect the correlational hypotheses with .97 power with the sample size of N = 39.

For the difference hypotheses, we could achieve only .58 power, but based on the results from Study 1, we could expect larger effect sizes (d = .90), resulting in acceptable power (.86).

However, the power for the regression hypotheses was only .48. We conducted the main analyses for these hypotheses as preregistered, but the results should be interpreted with great caution. As in Study 1, we assessed intermediate deep-feature knowledge with three different measures: think-aloud, recognition test, and stimulated self-report. The think-aloud measure remained the same as in Study 1. Two trained raters double-coded about 25% of the protocols (ICC = .98). For the recognition test, we used the same three items showing defamiliarised diagrams as in Study 1. However, as the reasoning part did not improve the accuracy of results, it was removed (see Online Resource 7 for the entire test). Participants could therefore receive up to 3 points on the recognition test.

Procedure, Materials, and Measurements

As the results of Study 1 indicated that the wording of some of the self-report options were misleading, we revised the stimulated self-report (see Online Resource 8). Thus, we reformulated the answer options but not the items themselves. For example, we changed the questionnaire after the think-aloud task on consistency' and added a depiction of the study timeline (see Figure 3) to better stimulate the recall and help learners distinguish between the study phases.

Results

Preliminary Analyses

We tested for systematic differences between conditions regarding prior knowledge and self-reported commitment. We found no significant difference in learners' self-rated prior knowledge regarding the variance formula or measures of dispersion, both Us < 173.00, both Zs < -0.76, both ps > .444 (two-tailed). Learners' self-reported commitment while preparing and while working on the posttest did not differ between conditions, both ts (37) < -0.75, both ps > .462 (two-tailed).

Due to technical problems with the timer of the online posttest survey, which resulted in many missing values for some posttest items, we decided to analyse only the near-transfer items, as the problems mostly occurred with the far-transfer items. We used joint multiple imputation to impute the remaining missing values, as this method works best for small samples (McNeish, 2017). Table 7 presents the means and standard deviations for the intermediate deep-feature knowledge measures as well as the posttest and commitment per condition, and Table 8 presents the intercorrelations of these variables.

Convergent Validity (Hypotheses 1a and 1b)

We expected intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure to be strongly positively correlated with the recognition test (H1a) and the stimulated self-report (H1b). The think-aloud score was significantly correlated with the stimulated selfreport score, r = .41, p = .010 (two-tailed), but not with the recognition test score, r = .05, p = .777 (two-tailed).

Differences Between Preparation Activities -Sensitivity (Hypotheses 2a, 2b, and 2c)

We expected low-generative preparation to result in greater deep-feature knowledge than high-generative preparation and that this difference would be detected in the think-aloud measure (H2a), recognition test (H2b), and stimulated self-report (H2c). As expected, the low-generative condition elaborated significantly more deep features than the high-generative condition in the think-aloud measure, t(37) = -8.67, p < .001 (one-tailed), d = -2.78. This difference was also detected with the stimulated self-report, t(37) = -2.37, p = .012 (one-tailed), d = -0.76, but not with the recognition test, t(37) = 0.83, p = .206 (one-tailed), d = 0.26.

Regression -Predictive Validity (Hypotheses 3a, 3b, and 3c)

We expected that learners' intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the think-aloud procedure (H3a), recognition test (H3b), or stimulated self-report (H3c) would predict the learning outcomes. The regression with pooled multiple imputation estimates showed that the think-aloud measure did not significantly predict near transfer, F(1, 37) = 1.49, β = .20, p = .231, adjusted R² = .01. The recognition test score did not significantly predict near transfer, F(1, 37) = 0.05, β = .02, p = .888, adjusted R² < .01. However, the stimulated self-report score significantly predicted near transfer F(1, 37) = 6.39, β = .38, p = .015, adjusted R² = .12.

Discussion Study 2

In line with Study 1 and previous studies (Glogger-Frey et al., 2015; Glogger-Frey et al., 2017; Ockl & Glogger-Frey, 2026), we found that low-generative preparation with worked examples resulted in better intermediate deep-feature knowledge than high-generative preparation with an invention task, as measured with the think-aloud procedure. This difference was also detected for the stimulated self-report but not for the recognition test. As in Study 1, the recognition test score was not significantly correlated with the think-aloud measure, suggesting poor convergent validity. For the stimulated self-report, we found a medium, significant correlation with the think-aloud measure. Along with the finding that both measurements could detect the same difference in intermediate deep-feature knowledge when comparing high-and low-generative preparation, the results suggest that the stimulated selfreport is accurate and sensitive enough to replace the think-aloud procedure. However, the results of the two measurements differed in their ability to predict the learning outcomes.

Whereas the stimulated self-report score significantly predicted near transfer, the think-aloud score did not. However, due to the small sample size, the statistical power of the regression analyses was limited, meaning that the results should be interpreted with caution.

General Discussion

The present two studies examined the sensitivity and validity of two newly developed instruments for measuring intermediate deep-feature knowledge by comparing them with an established think-aloud measure. To assess convergent validity, we hypothesised that the thinkaloud measure would be strongly correlated with both the recognition test and the stimulated self-report. As an indicator of sensitivity, we compared high-generative and low-generative preparation activities. We expected the newly developed instruments and the think-aloud measure to provide similar results, thereby replicating previous findings that self-explaining worked examples results in better intermediate deep-feature knowledge (Glogger-Frey et al., 2022; Ockl & Glogger-Frey, 2026; Roelle & Berthold, 2015). Additionally, we investigated the predictive validity of the instruments by examining whether intermediate deep-feature knowledge could predict the learning outcomes.

Recognition Test

The basic idea behind the recognition test was similar to that of recall tests used in other studies (cf. Glogger-Frey et al., 2015; Glogger-Frey et al., 2017; Schwartz et al., 2011): If learners had elaborated on a deep feature during preparation and it had become part of their intermediate knowledge, they should be able to recognise or recall figures depicting these features. However, we found no correlation between the recognition test and the think-aloud measure in either study, indicating poor convergent validity. The recognition test was unable to detect differences between high-and low-generative preparation activities. These results differ from those obtained for the think-aloud measure, suggesting that the recognition test also shows poor sensitivity. Therefore, the recognition test is not an appropriate alternative to the think-aloud measure. To avoid confounding the learning process with a testing effect, the diagrams used in the recognition test were defamiliarised. This could explain the results, as the diagrams may have been defamiliarised to such an extent that learners were unable to recognise which deep-features were depicted. Therefore, a challenge for future studies is to find the balance between defamiliarised the diagrams sufficiently and avoiding a testing effect.

Stimulated Self-Report

The idea behind the stimulated self-report was to stimulate participants' recall of the moment when they first elaborated on a deep feature in order to make the self-report more accurate (cf. stimulated recall; Gass & Mackey, 2013). As expected, we found medium correlations between intermediate deep-feature knowledge measured with the stimulated selfreport and the think-aloud procedure. However, the adapted version used in Study 2 produced larger correlations, indicating that it is more effective in assessing intermediate deep-feature knowledge. This conclusion is also supported by the finding that only the adapted version could detect a difference between high-and low-generative preparation. This finding is consistent with those from the think-aloud measure and aligns with the results of other studies (Glogger-Frey et al., 2022; Ockl & Glogger-Frey, 2026; Roelle & Berthold, 2015). Our studies also demonstrated that the stimulation must be carefully designed to actually support the recall of thoughts in selfreports. The self-report only showed the expected results when learners were properly stimulated by a visualised timeline to help them differentiate between the study phases and small pictures of the material they had seen to help recall. In summary, the adapted version of the stimulated self-report showed acceptable convergent validity and sensitivity, making it an appropriate tool for measuring intermediate deep-feature knowledge.

Intermediate Deep-feature Knowledge and Learning Outcomes

Besides testing and validating the instruments, we also investigated whether intermediate deep-feature knowledge predicted the learning outcomes. Due to the small sample size in Study 2 and the resulting low statistical power of the regression analyses, we focus primarily on the Study 1 results. With the think-aloud measure, we found that intermediate deep-feature knowledge significantly predicted the near transfer of conceptual knowledge, but in the low-generative condition only. Learners who elaborated more on deep features while selfexplaining worked examples performed better in the near-transfer tasks. This finding aligns with previous studies that showed that self-explaining worked examples led to better intermediate deep-feature knowledge, which in turn positively affected near transfer (Glogger-Frey et al., 2022; Ockl & Glogger-Frey, 2026). Therefore, our findings support the theoretical assumption that deep-feature recognition is a mechanism of preparation-for-learning (Loibl et al., 2017).

Looking at far transfer as a learning outcome, we found that intermediate deep-feature knowledge did not predict performance on far-transfer items, regardless of the instrument used.

In other words, learners who elaborated more on deep features did not perform better on these items. One possible explanation is that the instructional video supported the development of conceptual knowledge that was sufficient for near-transfer tasks but did not extend to the demands of far transfer, which required learners to use this knowledge for argumentation (cf.

Ockl & Glogger-Frey, 2026). Thus, the effect of deep-feature processing may have been limited to developing a conceptual understanding without providing a sufficient basis for additional processes required for far transfer.

Overall, the think-aloud measure demonstrated good predictive validity in terms of near transfer but only when preparation induced higher levels of intermediate deep-feature knowledge, that is, in a low-generative worked example activity (Study 1). We suggest that a certain level of intermediate deep-feature knowledge is necessary to positively affect learning outcomes. In terms of the predictive validity of the developed instruments, the recognition test performed poorly, and the results did not converge with those from the think-aloud measure.

Therefore, the recognition test is not an appropriate replacement for the think-aloud procedure.

By contrast, the stimulated self-report showed greater potential, as the (more convincing, exploratory) stimulated self-report score from Study 1 produced findings comparable to those from the think-aloud measure, suggesting similar predictive validity. In Study 2, the adapted stimulated self-report demonstrated even stronger predictive validity compared with the thinkaloud measure. However, due to its limited statistical power, this finding should be interpreted with caution.

Generalisation and Limitations

We assume that the stimulated self-report can be adapted to other learning content and materials. For example, the contrasting cases on density with clowns and buses (cf. Schwartz et al., 2011) included the deep feature that the index needed to consider the ratio (of clowns to bus compartments). Therefore, the stimulated self-report could depict the learning sequence and simply ask learners if and when they considered the role of ratios in the density index. Even though we assume that the stimulated self-report measure can easily be adapted to different materials and topics, whether the instrument would be valid is an empirical question. When measuring intermediate deep-feature knowledge, it is crucial to identify the learning content's deep features. By using the variance, we chose a typical learning content with predefined functional components (Loibl et al., 2020), which can be considered the deep features. The learning content is an important basis for further research on mechanisms of problem-solving prior to instruction (Loibl et al., 2025). To measure intermediate deep-feature knowledge in other domains, its deep features would similarly need to be clearly defined before measurement so that a self-report item can be created for each deep feature.

Conclusion

When investigating the role of intermediate knowledge in preparation-for-learning, it is important to employ nonreactive, sensitive measurement instruments.

Investigating intermediate deep-feature knowledge and its influence on further learning processes is crucial for understanding how preparation-for-learning works, especially as deep-feature recognition is theorised as an underlying mechanism. Our study tested both a recognition test and a selfreport to demonstrate that investigations of intermediate deep-feature knowledge do not necessarily require time-consuming think-aloud procedures. Although the recognition test did not prove to be a sensitive or valid alternative, the stimulated self-report did. A well-designed stimulated self-report with clear response options can provide a more efficient and nonreactive alternative for assessing intermediate deep-feature knowledge.

The intermediate knowledge acquired during preparation influences subsequent learning processes and therefore plays a key role in understanding how preparation-for-learning works and what makes it effective. Whereas there are reliable and sensitive measures of knowledge-gap awareness, measuring intermediate knowledge about deep features is more complicated. If the measurement method is too direct, it can influence the learning process and obscure experimental variations, but if it is not direct enough, it may lack sensitivity or reliability.

Think about the tasks you have just completed. Indicate how committed you felt while working on them on a percentage scale ranging from 0% (not committed at all) to 100% (very committed).' Participants also self-assessed their prior knowledge on measures of dispersion with two items (e.g., 'I knew the formula for calculating the standard deviation before participating') with the answer options yes, no, and uncertain (see Online Resource 5).

Materials and measurements were very similar to those used in Study 1. The procedure also remained the same as in Study 1, except that the posttest was conducted entirely on a computer. The items used to assess prior knowledge and commitment while working on the task as well as the posttest and its rating scheme remained the same as in Study 1. Two trained raters double-coded about 25% of each open item (all ICCs > .75). The contrasting cases and preparation tasks used for the two experimental conditions were the same as in Study 1, but contextual information was removed. Whereas the diagrams in Study 1 depicted the amount of antioxidants in the tea of a fictional tea-grower, the diagrams in Study 2 did not specify what they depicted (see Online Resource 6). Therefore, the instructional video in Study 2 was adapted slightly to remove this contextual information.
